The crates/radicle-httpd subdirectory carries its own LICENSE-MIT and LICENSE-APACHE, so the daemon binary is dual-licensed, but anyone diligencing \"the stack is MIT/Apache\" will get a surprise on the frontend.\n\n8. THE CUSTOM-COB ESCAPE HATCH IS MUCH MORE EXPENSIVE THAN DESCRIBED, AND THE REPORT CONTRADICTS ITSELF. The report correctly warns \"Do not [reimplement COB reduction in Go]: the CRDT reduction ... is exactly the part that is subtle\" \u2014 then recommends defining your own checks COB. Read crates/radicle/src/cob/external.rs: an external COB type requires you to ship an executable HELPER that Radicle invokes, which \"implements the evaluation logic for that particular COB\", receives the operation plus a `concurrent` array of concurrent ops over JSON Lines on stdin, and returns the merged state on stdout. Defining com.acme.check means writing precisely the CRDT reduction the report tells you not to write \u2014 you just write it in a subprocess instead of in-process.\n\n9. The revision noun is real, immutable in the way that matters, individually addressable by a content-addressed operation OID, and carries its own base \u2014 that part of the report is correct and it is the thing GitHub cannot give you. What the report undersells is that everything on the write and notification side is a local CLI shell-out with no HTTP surface, no announce on the one command that can post inline comments, no per-ref cursor for your own writes, a silently-idempotent review action, and a custom-COB path that requires implementing CRDT merge in a helper binary. For a Go-based platform on one Kubernetes cluster, the reviewer bot must be co-located with a node holding the repo and a signing key, shell out to rad, chase every cob update with rad sync --announce, and hand-roll a Go mirror of the Action enum with lowercase verdicts. What genuinely blocks adoption is narrower and survives scrutiny: (1) no structured file/line/hunk field, (2) no verdict/approve/request-changes object, (3) no resolved flag, (4) no base commit recorded per round (only a mutable target branch at pull level, plus an appview-only source_rev that is the HEAD), (5) webhooks never fire on review arrival so requirement #3 still needs a firehose cursor, (6) alpha with per-release migrations, and (7) a two-language extension surface (Go appview + Rust bobbin), not the pure-Go story claimed. The report's final INFERRED note \u2014 that Tangled is the most plausible fork/extend candidate \u2014 remains reasonable, and is somewhat strengthened by the feed.comment finding, since round-pinned threaded comments already exist and only the location and verdict fields would be new."

NOTE for the team's BASE problem: a patch set is addressed by its own commit SHA, and its base is that commit's parent \u2014 recoverable, but the base is NOT a first-class named field the way Radicle's is. https://gerrit-review.googlesource.com/Documentation/concept-patch-sets.html",

(c) The recommended replacement is weaker than it sounds: the Checks API (https://gerrit-review.googlesource.com/Documentation/pg-plugin-checks-api.html) is a FRONTEND JavaScript plugin API \u2014 a browser-side plugin implements fetch() returning Promise<FetchResponse>; it is NOT a server-side REST surface for posting results. (d) The plugin that DID provide a checks REST API is dead: GerritCodeReview/plugins_checks carries a \"DEPRECATION NOTICE\" stating \"The Gerrit team at Google has decided to discontinue work on the checks plugin.\" Net effect: to post CI/check results you must either write and host your own JS plugin + backend, or keep using review messages/labels.",

This is the one that satisfies \"no mirrored cursor\". (2) `ssh stream-events` \u2014 real-time JSON, one event per line, requires the 'Stream Events' global capability.           "anchoring": "BEST DOCUMENTED ANSWER IN THE INDUSTRY \u2014 and it explicitly documents its own failure modes, which is what the team asked for. Documented fallback ladder, verbatim: \"Gerrit tries to calculate the position of this comment on the new version of the file... It's not always possible to calculate an appropriate position for a comment. In this case, Gerrit attaches these comments as File Level Comments. In some exceptional cases (such as the entire file being reverted), there is no appropriate file to associate this comment with. In this case we do not port this comment over. The comment is still present at its original location and visible in the Comments Tab & Change Log.\" Algorithm confirmed in source (java/com/google/gerrit/server/restapi/change/CommentPorter.java, 458 lines): GitPositionTransformer parameterised with BestPositionOnConflict.INSTANCE, driven by DiffMappings/FileMapping between the two patchset commits; on any exception loading mappings it logs a warning and falls back to getFallbackMappings(comments). It even ships observability: counters ported_comments/as_file_level and ported_comments/as_range_comments. CAVEAT the team must know: CommentPorter is typed to HumanComment and ListPortedComments calls publishedHumanCommentsByChange \u2014 ROBOT COMMENTS WERE NEVER PORTED. Endpoints: GET .../ported_comments, GET .../ported_drafts.",

          "machine_reviewer_api": "The team's exact bug \u2014 'the agent's reply to a review comment is filed under a NEW review object with non-null submitted_at, so the platform self-triggers' \u2014 has FOUR known mitigations, of which only two are server-side. (1) GitHub Actions, SERVER-SIDE and the strongest: \"When you use the repository's GITHUB_TOKEN to perform tasks, events triggered by the GITHUB_TOKEN will not create a new workflow run\", with documented exceptions \u2014 workflow_dispatch and repository_dispatch always create runs, and a PR opened/synchronize/reopened by GITHUB_TOKEN creates runs in an approval-required state rather than being blocked. This only helps if your consumer IS a workflow; it does nothing for an external service on webhooks. (2) Gerrit, SERVER-SIDE-ish and the best fit for an external consumer: every review carries a free-form `tag`; the `autogenerated:` prefix is the established convention (Zuul emits autogenerated:ci-test, autogenerated:gate-and-submit) and Gerrit's own UI hides those messages. Pair with the `serviceuser` plugin so the bot identity is structurally distinct \u2014 service users cannot log into the WebUI and cannot push commits or tags. A consumer filters on tag prefix AND author account. (3) Probot, CLIENT-SIDE ONLY: the community answer is to filter payload.sender.type === 'Bot' or match sender.login against your own app. Notably probot/probot issue #63 requested this as a DEFAULT and it remains a userland filter \u2014 i.e. the framework never adopted it. (4) Radicle, STRUCTURAL: every COB operation is cryptographically signed by a keypair, so the agent's own writes are identifiable by author key with no heuristic at all.",

      "notes": "WHAT DOES NOT EXIST \u2014 stated confidently, backed by search evidence.\n\n1. NOBODY HAS BUILT A REVIEW SYSTEM FOR MACHINE AUTHORS. I searched the requested terms and variants (\"agent code review platform open source\", \"AI agent pull request alternative\", \"change management for AI agents\", \"review layer for coding agents\", \"agent-native forge\", \"agent authored changes\", plus \"self-hosted forge revision object for AI agent authored change\"). Every named open-source project that surfaced is agent-as-REVIEWER, not agent-as-AUTHOR: PR-Agent/Qodo, CodeRabbit, Gito, kodus, alibaba/open-code-review, agentuse/pr-review-agent, spencermarx/open-code-review, ellenhp/auditlm (Forgejo), GerritForge/ai-review-agent-provider. All of them post LLM commentary onto an existing PR/MR/change. NONE introduces a change object, a revision series, or an identity model. The category the team is asking for is empty.\n\n2. THE SEARCH NOISE IS ITSELF THE FINDING. \"AI code review\" has been so thoroughly colonised by review-bot products that the inverse problem \u2014 what the change object should look like when the AUTHOR is a machine \u2014 has essentially no open-source representation. That asymmetry is worth reporting to stakeholders on its own.\n\n3. NO MAJOR FORGE SHIPPED ANYTHING FOR AGENT AUTHORS IN 2025-2026.\n   - Gerrit: shipped the \"Review Agent\" in 3.14 (2026-05-15) \u2014 an AI chat sidebar for humans, requiring an AiCodeReviewProvider plugin; the reference implementation, GerritForge/ai-review-agent-provider, is BSL 1.1 (source-available, NOT open source) and needs Gerrit 3.14.x+. Meanwhile Gerrit is DELETING robot comments, the one machine-authorship primitive it had.\n   - GitLab: Duo is proprietary EE; nothing in CE; no new change/revision object.\n   - Forgejo: moved the opposite way and banned AI-generated work outright (March 2026 report).\n   - GitHub: its own guidance, \"Agent pull requests are everywhere. Here's how to review them.\", is PURE WORKFLOW ADVICE on existing PR infrastructure \u2014 scan/classify, check CI changes, hunt duplicate utilities, \"Let Copilot review it first\". It proposes NO new data model, revision object, or API for agent authors. GitHub, which has the most agent-authored PRs on earth, is not fixing this at the model layer.\n\n4. THE BEST-FUNDED ATTEMPT HAS NOT SHIPPED THE HARD PART. Entire (Thomas Dohmke, ex-GitHub CEO, $60M seed at $300M, out of stealth 2026-02-10) is premised on exactly the team's thesis \u2014 \"Pull requests still assume a human wrote every line and can explain every decision.\" Six months later the only public artifact is Checkpoints, an MIT Go CLI that stores agent sessions on an `entire/checkpoints/v1` branch. That is provenance capture, not a change model. Read this as corroboration that the revision-noun layer is unbuilt and hard, not as a solution to adopt.\n\n5. ACADEMIC WORK IS VISION-ONLY. arXiv 2605.17548, \"Rethinking Code Review in the Age of AI: A Vision for Agentic Code Review\" (Kamal\u0131, Tuna, Haratian, T\u00fcz\u00fcn; submitted 2026-05-17, rev. 2026-06-05; targeting TOSEM, short version at ICSE-JAWs 2026), proposes a five-stage lifecycle (PR creation, augmentation, reviewer selection, AI-assisted review, retrospective) layered on PRs \u2014 explicitly NO new data model and NO shipped system. arXiv 2604.24450 empirically studies 7,416 reviewer-bot comments across 4,532 agentic PRs and finds a \"dilution effect\" (more bot comments, lower average relevance; higher volume correlates with longer resolution, rho=0.19) but does not touch self-triggering or the adequacy of the PR model for agent authors.\n\n6. COMMENT-ANCHOR FORWARDING: EXACTLY ONE REAL, PUBLISHED, OPEN-SOURCE ALGORITHM EXISTS \u2014 GERRIT'S. This is the highest-value concrete finding in my area and it is directly reusable regardless of which forge the team picks. Gerrit does NOT re-anchor; it PORTS, keeping one comment shown in two places (\"Ported comments are not copies of the comment but the comment simply shown in another place\"; resolving a ported comment updates the original). Eligibility: unresolved comments, unresolved drafts, resolved drafts \u2014 resolved comments are deliberately not ported. Documented three-rung fallback ladder: computed position -> File Level Comment when no position can be calculated -> NOT PORTED AT ALL when no appropriate file exists (e.g. whole file reverted), with the comment preserved at its original location and visible in the Comments Tab and Change Log. Implementation verified in source: java/com/google/gerrit/server/restapi/change/CommentPorter.java (458 lines) uses GitPositionTransformer parameterised with BestPositionOnConflict.INSTANCE over DiffMappings/FileMapping between the two patchset commits; on any mapping-load exception it logs and degrades via getFallbackMappings(); it emits counters ported_comments/as_file_level and ported_comments/as_range_comments. TRAP THE TEAM MUST KNOW: CommentPorter is typed to HumanComment and ListPortedComments calls publishedHumanCommentsByChange \u2014 ROBOT COMMENTS ARE NEVER PORTED. If JetBridge's agent writes machine comments and Gerrit's robot-comment type is also being removed, machine comments must be written as ordinary human-type comments (from a service user, tagged autogenerated:) or they get no anchor forwarding at all.\n   Everything else is closed or unpublished: Reviewable (proprietary SaaS) markets that it \"maps line comments across file revisions and keeps them around until resolved\" and that a comment survives rebase/amend, but publishes no algorithm. Phabricator/Phorge sidesteps the problem by binding comments to immutable Diffs rather than forwarding them. GitHub/GitLab/Forgejo simply mark comments outdated with no forwarding and no documented fallback. I found NO research paper presenting a comment-anchor-forwarding algorithm.\n\n7. SEALED / CONTENT-ADDRESSED REVIEW RECORDS OUTSIDE RADICLE: essentially one serious precedent, and it is dormant. google/git-appraise (Apache-2.0, Go, 5304 stars) stores reviews, comments, and CI results as git objects in dedicated refs \u2014 refs/notes/devtools/reviews, /discuss, /analyses, /ci \u2014 with no server at all. Its machine/human split (robot analyses in /analyses vs human review in /discuss) is direct prior art for the distinction the team needs. LAST PUSH 2023-08-12: three years dormant, reference implementation only. git-bug (GPL-3.0, Go, 9967 stars, v0.10.1 2025-05-19) applies the same philosophy to ISSUES, not code-review revisions. Fossil bundles tickets/wiki into the repository but has no revision-series review model. Sourcehut is the interesting near-miss: email patch series carry a natural revision noun by convention ([PATCH v2]), lists.sr.ht has a GraphQL API and GraphQL-native webhooks with delivery records retained 90+ days, but webhooks are read-only and cannot execute mutations, and the \"revision\" is a mailing-list convention rather than an enforced object.\n\n8. PRACTICAL BOTTOM LINE FOR THE TEAM'S DECISION. Nothing exists that is purpose-built for machine authors, so the choice is which system's EXISTING revision noun to adopt. Gerrit is the only self-hostable OSS system that has all four of: a true change identity independent of any commit, a bot-usable review-write API, real webhook push, and a published anchor-forwarding algorithm with documented failure modes. Its serious caveat is that its machine-reviewer story is mid-migration and partly abandoned (robot comments removed next release; the checks plugin that provided the REST replacement is explicitly discontinued; the surviving Checks API is frontend-only), so posting CI/check results means writing and hosting a JS plugin plus backend, or staying on review messages and labels. Radicle has by far the best DATA MODEL \u2014 Revision { id, base: Oid, head: Oid, discussion: Thread<Comment<CodeLocation>>, reviews, resolves }, immutable, content-addressed, signed \u2014 and its `base` field is the exact fix for sealing two 789 MiB worktrees per observation; but it has no webhooks, no Go client, and no published anchor forwarding. If the team ends up building rather than adopting, copy Radicle's Patch COB schema, Gerrit's porting ladder plus its as_file_level/as_range_comments metrics, Phabricator's immutable-Harbormaster-build check model, and ghstack's base/head/orig ref triple.\n\nCONFIDENCE AND CAVEATS. Facts marked DOCUMENTED are quoted from primary sources (official docs, release notes, READMEs) or verified directly against the GitHub API and the Go module proxy on 2026-08-06; repo metrics and release dates in this report come from those API calls, not from page summaries. Gerrit's porting behaviour was read from the project's own Documentation/user-porting-comments source and cross-checked against CommentPorter.java and ListPortedComments.java fetched from GerritCodeReview/gerrit master. Items marked INFERRED are my judgement. Two things I could NOT verify and that should be checked before any commitment: (a) whether go-gerrit covers the ported_comments/ported_drafts endpoints \u2014 assume you hand-roll them; (b) GitLab's retention guarantees for historical merge_request_diffs, which bear directly on whether MR versions can serve as a durable record."

        "system": "## Gerrit Code Review \u2014 adversarial verification verdict\n\n**Net: the report's bottom line (\"strong-fit\") survives, but for partly different reasons than it gives. It is WRONG in one direction that matters and STALE in another.**\n\nThree material corrections, in order of impact on the team's decision:\n\n**1. REFUTED \u2014 \"the base is NOT a first-class named field.\"** This is the report's stated weakness on the team's #1 requirement, and it is simply wrong. `RevisionInfo` carries `parents_data`, a list of `ParentInfo` entities returned with the `PARENTS` option, documented since at least stable-3.12. `ParentInfo` has named fields `commit_id`, `branch_name`, `is_merged_in_target_branch`, `change_id`, `change_number`, `patch_set_number`, `change_status`. `RevisionInfo` additionally carries a per-patchset `branch`, explicitly documented as differing per patchset after a Move Change. So each patch set records its own base \u2014 as a named, machine-readable field, per revision, including whether the base is another change's patch set. This directly addresses the \"a snapshot has a HEAD but a change has a BASE\" problem that cost them 789 MiB per observation. The report undersold Gerrit here.\n\n**2. STALE/OVERSTATED \u2014 robot comments are not \"being removed,\" they are GONE.** The report cites 3.12's \"will be removed with the next release\" and describes the feature as in-flight. That next release shipped nine months ago. Gerrit 3.13 release notes: \"Remove Robot Comments from Gerrit UI and the backend. Robot comments are not supported anymore and users have to move to the Checks API.\" Verified in source: `java/com/google/gerrit/entities/RobotComment.java` returns HTTP 200 on `stable-3.12` and 404 on `stable-3.13` and `master`. `grep -i robot` over the stable-3.14 REST API documentation returns zero hits. On 3.14 \u2014 the version the report recommends \u2014 the machine-reviewer-specific comment type does not exist at all. The report's caveat that robot comments were never ported is therefore moot.\n\n**3. REFUTED \u2014 the `autogenerated:` tag does not solve the self-trigger problem at the event layer.** The report presents tags as \"a real first-class one\" for self-trigger suppression. But the documented `comment-added` stream event schema is exactly: `change`, `patchSet`, `author`, `approvals`, `comment`, `eventCreatedOn`. **No `tag` field.** An external consumer of webhooks or stream-events cannot filter its own writes by tag, because the tag is not in the payload. The real answer is filtering on `author` (an account attribute), which the `serviceuser` plugin gives a stable identity for. The tag is a REST/UI-level affordance, and even there the report misdescribes it: the docs say autogenerated votes/comments \"can be filtered out in the web UI\" (a user-controlled filter), and the one *automatic* hiding rule is unrelated \u2014 \"If another message was posted on a newer patchset, but with the same tag, then the older message will be hidden in the UI.\" Practically the team is fine, but via a different mechanism than reported.\n\n**Sharper version of the checks-plugin problem.** The report says `plugins_checks` is \"dead.\" The DEPRECATION NOTICE quote is verbatim accurate, but \"dead\" is imprecise in both directions. The repo *is* still receiving commits (2026-07-31, \"Fix checks web tests for current plugin API\"). The genuinely damning fact the report missed: **its branch list ends at `stable-3.11`** \u2014 no `stable-3.12`, `stable-3.13`, or `stable-3.14`. Gerrit 3.11 went EOL 2026-05-15. The only implementation with a server-side checks REST API has no release branch for any currently-supported Gerrit. The report's conclusion (roll your own, or use review messages/labels) is correct and if anything understated.\n\n**Where the report is right and I could not break it:**\n- Anchoring. Every quote from `Documentation/user-porting-comments.txt` is verbatim-exact, including the eligibility list and the full fallback ladder. Every source claim about `CommentPorter.java` checks out: 458 lines, typed to `HumanComment`, `new GitPositionTransformer(BestPositionOnConflict.INSTANCE)`, `getFallbackMappings`, and the metrics. (It omits a third metric, `ported_comments/as_patchset_level`.)\n- Endpoint paths `/ported_comments` and `/ported_drafts` are exactly right.\n- Checks API really is a browser-side JS plugin API \u2014 `provider` must implement `fetch()` returning `Promise<FetchResponse>`. Not a server-side REST surface. Confirmed verbatim.\n- Health. v3.14.2 tagged 2026-07-13, v3.14.0 2026-05-15, v3.13.0 2025-11-10, v3.12.0 2025-05-19 \u2014 the ~6-month cadence holds. 150 commits in the last five weeks. No stable-3.15 branch yet.\n- webhooks: all five config knobs and per-event filtering confirmed.\n\n**One claim the report undersold in the team's favour, and one risk it missed.** Undersold: `CommentInput` accepts `fix_suggestions` (a list of `FixSuggestionInfo`), so a bot can post machine-authored suggested edits through the ordinary `POST .../review` endpoint \u2014 no plugin needed. Also, `webhooks` is a **core submodule** in `.gitmodules`, i.e. bundled, not third-party; whereas `serviceuser` and `events-log` are **not** core and must be sourced separately. Missed risk: `go-gerrit` still declares `RobotComments` in its `ReviewInput` (removed server-side in 3.13) and its `CommentInput` lacks `FixSuggestions` \u2014 the library trails the server by two releases on exactly the surface the team cares about.\n\n**Correction to ops_cost:** \"H2 for small installs or Postgres/NoteDb\" is confused. Since Gerrit 3.0 there is no relational ReviewDb at all. The single relational database is `accountPatchReviewDb`, which stores only \"the user file reviewed flags\"; its supported URLs are CLOUDSPANNER, H2, POSTGRESQL, MARIADB, MYSQL, defaulting to H2. Postgres is not an alternative store for review data. The report's *conclusion* \u2014 review data lives in git, not a relational mirror, which is what kills the 45-column mirror table \u2014 is correct.",

(3) `reviews` is `BTreeMap<ActorId, Review>`, one review slot per actor per revision, not a list; the report's flat \"reviews\" hides a real constraint on machine reviewers. The redact handler at patch.rs:1082-1099 sets `*r = None` \u2014 the revision is tombstoned out of the materialized patch. Guards exist but are narrow: the ROOT revision cannot be redacted, and an already-merged revision's redaction is ignored. Authorization (patch.rs:923-930) allows the revision author to edit or redact their own revision. What IS genuinely immutable is the (base, oid) pair \u2014 no action anywhere mutates a revision's base or head; a new code state requires a new Action::Revision with a new RevisionId. So the correct claim is: the CODE POINTER of a revision is immutable and content-addressed; the revision OBJECT is editable and redactable. The report's unqualified \"immutable\" oversells it, and \"sealed record\" needs the same qualification."

Author identity is carried structurally: Review.author: Author (patch.rs:1820) and every authorization check compares against a public key (e.g. patch.rs:911-914 `actor == comment.author() || actor == review.author.public_key() || actor == revision.author.public_key()`)."

Since Action derives Serialize/Deserialize with stable serde tags (\"review\", \"review.comment\", \"review.comment.resolve\", \"revision\", \"revision.edit\"...), a Go program can emit the FULL patch action surface as JSON \u2014 including inline `review.comment` with a `location` \u2014 without any Rust.         "system": "GitLab (CE/EE) \u2014 merge request diff versions. ADVERSARIAL VERIFICATION VERDICT: the reported writeup's bottom line (\"partial-fit\") survives, but roughly half its supporting claims are wrong in a way that matters, and it is wrong in BOTH directions \u2014 it undersells the revision noun and oversells the machine-reviewer API.\n\nWHAT THE REPORT GOT WRONG IN GITLAB'S FAVOR (i.e. the report was too harsh):\n1. \"GitLab has NO published anchor-forwarding algorithm comparable to Gerrit's CommentPorter\" is REFUTED. `lib/gitlab/diff/position_tracer.rb` exists in the MIT-licensed core and is invoked from `MergeRequests::ReloadDiffsService` (`update_diff_discussion_positions`). It dispatches to LineStrategy/FileStrategy/ImageStrategy and traces a comment position across three diff ranges (ac/bd/cd). The correct criticism is that it is UNDOCUMENTED (no user- or API-level description, no documented fallback ladder), not that it does not exist.\n2. \"reviews/approvals attach to the MR rather than to a version\" is OVERSTATED. `Approval` carries `sha_attribute :patch_id_sha` plus a `with_invalid_patch_id_sha` scope \u2014 approvals are content-addressed and invalidated when the patch identity changes (so they survive a pure rebase). And inline review comments REQUIRE `position[base_sha]`, `position[start_sha]`, `position[head_sha]` \u2014 i.e. every diff note records exactly the three SHAs that define one version. What is genuinely absent is a `review` object and a `merge_request_diff_id` FK; the binding is by SHA/patch-id, not by version row.\n3. The report missed `patch_id_sha` on the version entity itself. That is the closest thing in any mainstream forge to a stable, content-derived revision identity, and it is the field the team would actually key on.\n4. Versions DO record their own base, explicitly: `base_commit_sha` (merge base), `start_commit_sha` (target-branch tip), `head_commit_sha`, all set once via `||=` in `ensure_commit_shas`. Requirement #1's \"each naming its own base\" is satisfied at the API level. The report stated this but buried it.\n5. MR webhook `action` values include `approval`, `approved`, `unapproval`, `unapproved` \u2014 a real verdict push signal the report did not mention.\n\nWHAT THE REPORT GOT WRONG IN GITLAB'S DISFAVOR (i.e. the report was too generous):\n6. \"external status checks for CI verdicts, all bot-usable\" is REFUTED for the OSS edition. External status checks are Ultimate-only. The CE-available equivalent is the commit status API (`POST /projects/:id/statuses/:sha`), which is Free but attaches to a SHA, not to an MR, and cannot block merge.\n7. \"approvals\" as listed under the machine-reviewer API is OVERSTATED. Approve/unapprove/reset/approval_state are Free, but they are advisory: \"These approvals are optional and don't prevent merging without approval.\" Required-approval rules are Premium+.\n8. \"immutable ... merge_request_diffs row\" is OVERSTATED, and the report's own INFERRED pruning warning should be upgraded to CONFIRMED-IN-SOURCE and made much louder. `MergeRequests::PostMergeService` calls `DeleteNonLatestDiffsService`, which batches `non_latest_diffs.with_files` (BATCH_SIZE = 10, staggered `index * 5.minutes`) into `DeleteDiffFilesWorker`, which `delete_all`s the `MergeRequestDiffFile` rows and transitions the diff to state `without_files` \u2014 whose source comment reads \"Diff files have been deleted by the system\". The row and its SHAs survive; the reviewed CONTENT is destroyed, and it is destroyed AT MERGE, exactly the moment a sealed record becomes valuable. For a platform whose whole point is an auditable revision series, this is the disqualifying fact, and it is by design, not a config accident.\n9. Undisclosed hard cap the report missed entirely: 1000 diff versions per MR. \"Merge requests that reach this limit cannot be updated further. Instead, close the affected merge request and create a new merge request.\" Introduced 17.10 behind `merge_requests_diffs_limit`, GA in 19.0. `ReloadDiffsService` silently returns early on `reached_versions_limit?` \u2014 a push past the cap produces no version and the change is not visible on the MR. An agent loop that revises aggressively can hit a wall that requires abandoning the change object.\n10. Go client advice is STALE AND WRONG TO ACT ON. v1.46.0 (2026-03-01) is superseded: `gitlab.com/gitlab-org/api/client-go/v2` is at v2.56.0, published 2026-08-05. Starting a new integration on the v1 import path would be a mistake. (License is Apache-2.0, which the report omitted.)\n11. \"no review submitted event\" is the real webhook gap, and the report didn't identify it. GitLab has no review object at all, so a batch of draft comments published together arrives as N independent `note` events. Also, webhook delivery is explicitly best-effort \u2014 receivers are auto-disabled after repeated failures \u2014 so the team still needs a reconciliation path, which partly defeats requirement #3's \"without maintaining a mirrored cursor.\"\n12. \"GitLab shipped NOTHING for agent AUTHORS in 2025-2026\" is REFUTED. Duo Agent Platform reached GA (announced 2026-01-15); the \"Developer (Issue to Merge Request)\" flow authors MRs, and the documented External Agents feature lets a self-managed third-party agent \"run a CI/CD pipeline and respond inside GitLab with either a ready-to-merge change or an inline comment,\" with custom external agents creatable on Self-Managed behind a feature flag. The parts of the report's conclusion that survive: it is Premium/Ultimate (never Free), it is proprietary, and it adds no change object \u2014 so it does not solve the team's problem, but the factual claim as written is false.\n\nCLAIMS I COULD NOT CHECK: \"HIGHEST ops cost of every option\" is a comparative ranking against options not in scope here \u2014 UNVERIFIABLE as stated. The absolute number is documented: the chart is \"intended to fit in a cluster with at least 8 vCPU and 30 GB of RAM,\" with a 3 vCPU / 12 GB minimal GKE example for non-production. That is heavy but not unbounded.\n\nNET RECOMMENDATION AGAINST THE TEAM'S SIX REQUIREMENTS:\n(1) Revision noun \u2014 better than reported (per-push row, own base SHAs, content-addressed patch_id_sha) but fails the DURABILITY test outright because diff files are deleted on merge, and fails on the 1000-version cap. A revision you can name but cannot re-read is not a sealed record.\n(2) Machine reviewer API \u2014 good for threaded comments and resolve (Free), weak for verdicts in CE (advisory approvals; commit statuses attach to a SHA; blocking status checks are Ultimate).\n(3) Push events \u2014 good, plus approval actions, but no review event and best-effort delivery.\n(4) Self-hostable and healthy \u2014 yes, unambiguously (19.2 shipped 2026-07-16, monthly cadence, MIT Expat core).\n(5) Anchoring \u2014 an implementation exists (PositionTracer) and is better than the report claims, but is undocumented, so it is an unspecified dependency, not a contract.\n(6) Go client \u2014 strong, but use v2.\nSustain \"partial-fit,\" and if the team writes this up, the headline reason should be diff-file deletion at merge plus the version cap, NOT the per-push granularity the report led with.",

      {
        "system": "Bot self-trigger suppression (cross-forge prior art) \u2014 ADVERSARIAL RE-VERIFICATION\n\nVERDICT ON THE REPORT: the catalogue is directionally right but its headline recommendation is broken. Two of its four mechanisms check out as described (GitHub's GITHUB_TOKEN rule; Radicle's signed COB operations). The one it names as \"the best fit for an external consumer\" \u2014 Gerrit's `autogenerated:` tag \u2014 does NOT work over Gerrit's push/event surface, which is exactly the topology the team asked about. I would downgrade this entry from \"partial-fit\" to \"useful taxonomy, one load-bearing recommendation refuted.\"\n\nTHE CENTRAL REFUTATION (item 2, Gerrit). The report says: \"A consumer filters on tag prefix AND author account.\" Half of that is impossible over webhooks or stream-events. Gerrit's `tag` lives on the REST-side entities (ChangeMessageInfo / CommentInfo) \u2014 it is NOT a field on the event objects. `CommentAddedEvent` (java/com/google/gerrit/server/events/CommentAddedEvent.java) declares exactly four members: `type`, `author`, `approvals`, `comment`; its ancestors `PatchSetEvent` / `ChangeEvent` / `RefEvent` / `Event` add `patchSet`, `change`, `project`, `refName`, `changeKey`, `eventCreatedOn`, `instanceId`. No `tag`, anywhere in the events package; `StreamEventsApiListener.java` never sets one. The `webhooks` core plugin propagates these same event objects. So an external Go service on webhooks sees only the author account \u2014 to see the tag it must call back into `GET /changes/{id}/messages` or `/comments`, i.e. re-introduce exactly the mirrored-cursor polling the team is trying to eliminate (their priority #3). The author-account check alone is not new information: GitHub gives them `sender.login` too.\n\nWHAT THE REPORT UNDERSTATES. The `autogenerated:` convention is more first-class than claimed. It is a core constant \u2014 `ChangeMessagesUtil.AUTOGENERATED_TAG_PREFIX = \"autogenerated:\"` \u2014 and Gerrit tags its own machine-written messages `autogenerated:gerrit:merged`, `autogenerated:gerrit:newPatchSet`, `autogenerated:gerrit:abandon`, and ~14 more. So the prefix is not merely \"an established convention\"; it is Gerrit's own internal marker with a helper (`isAutogenerated`). Credit where due \u2014 but it changes nothing about the event-payload gap above.\n\nWHAT THE REPORT OVERSTATES OR GETS WRONG.\n- \"Zuul emits autogenerated:ci-test, autogenerated:gate-and-submit\" \u2014 those literals do not exist in Zuul. Zuul emits `autogenerated:zuul:<pipeline-name>` (so, on OpenDev, `autogenerated:zuul:check` / `autogenerated:zuul:gate`), hardcoded in two places in `gerritconnection.py`, gated on `self.version >= (2, 13, 0)`. It is not a configurable reporter option: `gerritreporter.py` contains zero occurrences of \"tag\" and its schema exposes only `comment`, `submit`, `checks-api`, `notify`, plus labels. And Zuul does not consume the tag for self-suppression \u2014 it filters triggers on approvals/comment regexes.\n- \"Gerrit's own UI hides those messages\" \u2014 no. The UI hides a tagged message only when a message with the SAME tag and a HIGHER revision number exists (`computeIsImportant` in gr-messages-list.ts), and the predicate keys off any non-empty `tag`, not the `autogenerated:` prefix. A single CI comment on the current patchset is shown. There is a \"show all activity\" toggle defaulting to hidden. Gerrit's own docs say tagged comments \"can be filtered out in the web UI\" \u2014 permissive, not automatic.\n- \"service users cannot log into the WebUI and cannot push commits or tags\" is a verbatim quote from the plugin's about.md, but the plugin's own config.md contradicts it as behavior: blocking is admin-configured, and the documented example explicitly leaves tag-pushing open (\"blocking push on `refs/heads/*` and `refs/meta/config` would prevent service users from pushing commits, while they still may push tags\"). Nothing there addresses `refs/for/*`, which is what an agent must push to upload a patchset \u2014 practically good news for the team, but it means \"structurally distinct\" is not what the plugin gives you out of the box.\n- The report does not say so, but `serviceuser` is NOT a bundled core plugin. Gerrit's `.gitmodules` core set is: codemirror-editor, commit-message-length-validator, delete-project, download-commands, gitiles, hooks, plugin-manager, replication, reviewnotes, singleusergroup, webhooks. serviceuser is a separate optional install. Health is fine \u2014 last commit 2026-06-09, steady activity through late 2025.\n- \"Probot ... the community answer is to filter payload.sender.type === 'Bot'\" understates it: Probot ships `context.isBot` as a documented getter in src/context.ts (`this.payload.sender?.type === \"Bot\"`). The substantive point survives \u2014 it is opt-in, not a default \u2014 but this is a framework API, not folklore.\n- Radicle framed as \"STRUCTURAL ... with no heuristic at all\" is a distinction of verifiability, not of mechanism. It is still an author-identity check, the same category as `sender.login`; the difference (a signature you can verify without trusting the server) buys nothing for a single-team self-hosted cluster. Worse for this use case: Radicle has no core event stream to suppress. External consumption requires the separate `radicle-ci-broker` plus a webhooks adapter.\n\nWHAT THE REPORT MISSES, AND IT MATTERS FOR A SELF-HOSTING TEAM. It presents GitHub Actions as the sole server-side example. Forgejo/Gitea Actions document an identical rule \u2014 \"In order to avoid infinite recursion, no workflow will be triggered as a side effect of a change authored with this token\" \u2014 and Forgejo is self-hostable and open-source, i.e. it is in the team's actual candidate set. GitLab documents the same class of rule for `CI_JOB_TOKEN` pushes. The mechanism is a commodity across forges, not a GitHub differentiator. The caveat the report correctly raises about GitHub applies verbatim to all three: it suppresses CI RUNS, not webhook deliveries, so it is worth nothing to an external consumer.\n\nBOTTOM LINE FOR THE TEAM. The report's final inference is the part that holds up best and should be promoted to the headline: the self-trigger fix is theirs to own \u2014 a dedicated bot identity plus a marker on every machine write. But the marker must be one the CHOSEN FORGE PUTS IN THE EVENT PAYLOAD. On Gerrit the tag is not, so the only field they can filter on push-side is the author account. That is fine and sufficient, but it means \"adopt Gerrit's tag convention\" is not the answer to their self-trigger problem; \"use a dedicated account and filter on author in the webhook\" is, and that answer was already available on GitHub.",
